0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}\!_{\mathit{be}}
can be found as follows. Since
F
implies
Q
,
the formula
F\wedge\overline{Q}
is unsatisfiable (and so
M

cannot produce an output falsifying
Q
). Let
F^{\prime}
be a formula
obtained by removing some clauses from
F
for which the formula
F^{\prime}\wedge\overline{Q}
is satisfiable. Then means that the circuit
M^{\prime}

specified by
F^{\prime}
can produce an output falsifying
Q
. The intuition
here is that if
Q
is unwanted property, the clauses removed from
F

to obtain
F^{\prime}
relate to the buggy part of
M
. (Note that the circuit

M^{\prime}
specified by
F^{\prime}
is non-deterministic. The reason is that
F^{\prime}
can
be satisfied by assignments (

Appendix 0.E
Combining PQE With Clause Splitting

In this appendix, we consider combining PQE with clause splitting
mentioned in Subsection
3.3
. We show that the
corresponding PQE problem of taking out a clause produced by splitting
is solved by
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
in linear time. We also show that if this clause is
not redundant, the solution produced by
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
is a single-test
property.

Here we reuse the notation of Section
3
but, for the
sake of simplicity, consider a single-output combinational circuit.
Let
M(X,V,w)
be such a circuit where
X
and
V
specify the
internal and input variables respectively and
w
is the output
variable of
M
. Let
F(X,V,w)
be a formula specifying the circuit

M
and
C
be a clause of
F
. Consider the case of
splitting

C
on
all
variables of
V
. That is
C=C_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge C_{p+1}
where
C_{1}=C\vee\overline{l(v_{1})}
,…,
C_{p}=C\vee\overline{l(v_{p})}
,
C_{p+1}=C\vee l(v_{1})\vee\dots\vee l(v_{p})
and
l(v_{i})
is a literal of

v_{i}
and
V=\mbox{$\{v_{1},\dots,v_{p}\}$}
. Let
F^{\prime}
denote the formula
obtained from
F
by replacing the clause
C
with
C_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge C_{p+1}
. Denote by
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}\!_{\mathit{spl}}
the input assignment
falsifying the literals
l(v_{1}),\dots,l(v_{p})
where ’spl’ stands for
’splitting’.

Consider applying
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
to solve the PQE problem of taking the clause

C_{p+1}
out of
\exists{X}[F^{\prime}]
.

\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
starts with looking for
an assignment satisfying
(F^{\prime}\setminus\mbox{$\{C_{p+1}\}$})\wedge\overline{C_{p+1}}
(to find a subspace where
F^{\prime}\setminus\mbox{$\{C_{p+1}\}$}
does not imply
C_{p+1}
). Consider the following three
cases. The first case is that the formula above is unsatisfiable. Then

C_{p+1}
is trivially redundant in
F^{\prime}
and hence in
\exists{X}[F^{\prime}]
and

\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
terminates.

The second case is that there is an assignment
(
\mbox{$\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}\!_{\mathit{spl}}$},w^{*})
. Since,
F^{\prime}\setminus\mbox{$\{C_{p+1}\}$}
is
satisfiable in this subspace,

is
not
redundant in

\exists{X}[F^{\prime}]
. To
make

redundant in subspace

,

. The clause

.

The clause

above is implied by

(and hence

property
of

. This property specifies the input/output
behavior of

under the input

when the variables of

. The latter is the output

under a

. In all three

.) So,

-

Appendix 0.F
Tests Specified By A Property Generated By PQE

In this appendix, we show the relation between tests specified by a
property obtained via PQE (see Subsection
3.5
) and
those detecting stuck-at faults. Here, we reuse the notation of
Section
3
. Let
M(X,V,W)
be a combinational circuit
where
X,V,W
are the internal, input and output variables
respectively. Let
F(X,V,W)
be a formula specifying
M
.
Let
G
be an AND gate of
M
whose functionality is
x_{3}=x_{1}\wedge x_{2}
. That is
x_{1},x_{2}
are the input variables of
G
and
x_{3}
is
its output variable. The functionality of
G
is specified by the
formula
C_{1}\wedge C_{2}\wedge C_{3}
where
C_{1}=\overline{x}_{1}\vee\overline{x}_{2}\vee x_{3}
,
C_{2}=x_{1}\vee\overline{x}_{3}
,
C_{3}=x_{2}\vee\overline{x}_{3}
(see Example
2
). The clauses

C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
are present in formula
F
. Consider taking
C_{1}
out of

\exists{X}[F]
. This clause makes
G
produce the output value 1 when its
input values are 1. (If
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are set to 1, the clause

C_{1}
can be satisfied only by setting
x_{3}
to 1.)

Let
H(V,W)
be the property obtained by taking out
C_{1}
. That
is
\mbox{$\exists{X}[F]$}\equiv H\wedge\mbox{$\exists{X}[F\setminus\mbox{$\{C_{1}\}$}]$}
. Let
Q(V,W)
be a clause of
H
. As we mentioned earlier,
we assume that
H
does not have redundant clauses i.e. those implied
by
F\setminus\mbox{$\{C_{1}\}$}
. Then the formula
(F\setminus\mbox{$\{C_{1}\}$})\wedge\overline{Q}
is satisfiable. Let (

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle v\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle w\hfil

) be
an assignment satisfying this formula. Note that this assignment

falsifies

C_{1}
. (Indeed, assume the contrary. Then
(

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle v\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle w\hfil

) satisfies
F
because it already satisfies

F\setminus\mbox{$\{C_{1}\}$}
. Since this assignment falsifies
Q
, we have to
conclude that
F\not\Rightarrow Q
and hence
F\not\Rightarrow H
. So we have a
contradiction.)

The fact that (

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle v\hfil

,

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle w\hfil

) falsifies
C_{1}
and satisfies

F\setminus\mbox{$\{C_{1}\}$}
means that one can view this assignment as an
execution trace of a faulty version
\mathit{M}_{\mathit{flt}}
of
M
. Namely, the
output
x_{3}
of gate
G
is stuck at 0 in
\mathit{M}_{\mathit{flt}}
. (The clause

C_{1}
is falsified when
x_{1}=1,x_{2}=1,x_{3}=0
i.e. if the gate
G

outputs 0 when its input variables are assigned 1.) Let

(\mbox{$\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern \mathit{DS}
-
\mathit{PQE}
has three situations where an atomic D-sequent is generated.
First, when
C
is blocked in the current subspace and hence is
redundant there. Then an atomic D-sequent
(\mbox{$\exists{X}[F]$},\mbox{$\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}$})\rightarrow C
is generated where

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle p\hfil

consists of assignments that
made
C
blocked in the current subspace. For instance, in
Example
3
,
\mathit{DS}
-
\mathit{PQE}
would generate an atomic D-sequent

(\mbox{$\exists{X}[F]$},(y_{1}\!=\!1))\rightarrow C
. Second, an atomic
D-sequent is generated when
C
is satisfied by an assignment
w=b

where
w\in X\cup Y
and
b\in\mbox{$\{0,1\}$}
. (This can be a decision
assignment or an assignment derived from a clause during BCP.) Then an
atomic D-sequent
(\mbox{$\exists{X}[F]$},(w=b))\rightarrow C
is
built. Third, an atomic D-sequent is generated when a conflict occurs
and a conflict clause
\mathit{C}_{\mathit{cnfl}}
falsified in the current subspace
is derived. Adding
\mathit{C}_{\mathit{cnfl}}
to
F
makes
C
redundant in the
current subspace. So, an atomic D-sequent
(\mbox{$\exists{X}[F]$},\mbox{$\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern is generated where

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle p\hfil

is the shortest assignment
falsifying
\mathit{C}_{\mathit{cnfl}}
.

Appendix 0.H
Experiments With HWMCC-13 Benchmarks

In Section
8
, we described experiments with
multi-property benchmarks of the HWMCC-13 set
[
19
]
. In this
appendix, we provide some additional information. Each benchmark
consists of a sequential circuit
N
and invariants
P_{0},\dots,P_{m}

that are supposed to hold for
N
. We will refer to the invariant

\mathit{P}_{\mathit{agg}}
equal to
P_{0}\wedge\dots\wedge P_{m}
as the

aggregate invariant
. We applied PQE to the generation of
invariants of
N
that may be
unwanted
. Since every invariant

P
implied by
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{agg}}

must
hold, the necessary condition
for
P
to be unwanted is
\mbox{$\mathit{P}_{\mathit{agg}}$}\not\Rightarrow P
.

Similarly to the experiment of Section
7
, we used the
formula
F_{k}=I(S_{0})\wedge T(S_{0},V_{0},S_{1})

\wedge\dots\wedge T(S_{k-1},V_{k-1},S_{k})
to generate invariants. The number
k
of time
frames was in the range of
2\!\leq\!k\!\leq\!10
. Specifically, we set
k
to the largest value in this
range where
|F_{k}|
did not exceed 500,000 clauses. We discarded the
benchmarks with
|F_{2}|\!>\!500,000
. We also dropped the smallest
benchmarks. So, in the experiments, we used 98 out of the 178
benchmarks of the set.

We describe three experiments. In every experiment, we generated
properties
H(S_{k})
by taking out a clause of
\exists{\mbox{$X_{k}$}}[F_{k}]

where
\mbox{$X_{k}$}\!=\!S_{0}\cup V_{0}\cup\dots\cup S_{k-1}\cup V_{k-\!1}
. Property
H
is a
local invariant
claiming that no
state falsifying
H
can be reached in
k
transitions. As in the
experiment of Section
7
, we took out only clauses
containing an unquantified variable (i.e a variable of
S_{k}
). In all
experiments, the
time limit
for solving a PQE problem was set to
10 sec.

0.H.1
Experiment 1

The objective of the
first experiment
was to demonstrate that

\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
could compute
H
for realistic designs. We also showed in this
experiment that PQE could be much easier than QE (see
Section
8
) and that
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
outperforms
\mathit{DS}
-
\mathit{PQE}
and

\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
. In this experiment, for each benchmark out of 98 mentioned above
we generated PQE problems of taking a clause out of

\exists{\mbox{$X_{k}$}}[F_{k}]
. Some of them were trivially solved by
preprocessing. The latter eliminated the blocked clauses of
F_{k}
that
were easy to identify and ran BCP launched due to the unit clauses
specifying the initial state. In all experiments, we
discarded

problems solved by preprocessing i.e. we considered only

non-trivial
PQE problems.

Let
C
be a clause taken out of
\exists{\mbox{$X_{k}$}}[F_{k}]
. We used
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
to
partition the PQE problems we tried into two groups.
The first
group
consisted of problems for which we ran
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
with the time
limit of 10 sec. and it never encountered a subspace

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle s_{k}\hfil

where

F_{k}
was satisfiable. Here

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle s_{k}\hfil

is a full assignment to

S_{k}
. (Recall that the variables of
S_{k}
are the only unquantified
variables of
\exists{\mbox{$X_{k}$}}[F_{k}]
.) So, in every subspace

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle s_{k}\hfil

tried by
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
, formula
F_{k}
was either unsatisfiable or

(F_{k}\!\setminus\!\mbox{$\{C\}$})\!\Rightarrow\!C
.
The second group
consisted
of problems where
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
encountered subspaces where
F_{k}
was
satisfiable. For either group we generated up to 40 problems per
benchmark. For some benchmarks, the total number of non-trivial
problems generated for the first or second group was under 40. Many
PQE problems of either group had hundreds of thousands of variables.

\mathit{DS}
-
\mathit{PQE}

\mathit{EG}

\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}

\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}

\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
uses a satisfying assignment as a proof of
redundancy of the clause
C
in subspace

.

0.H.2
Experiment 2

The second experiment was an extension of the first one. Its goal was
to show that PQE can generate invariants for realistic designs. For
each clause
Q
of a local invariant
H
generated by PQE we used
IC3
to verify if
Q
was a global invariant. If so, we checked if

\mbox{$\mathit{P}_{\mathit{agg}}$}\not\Rightarrow Q
held. To make the experiment less time
consuming, in addition to the time limit of 10 sec. per PQE problem,
we imposed the following constraints. First, we stopped a PQE-solver
even before the time limit if it generated more than 5 free
clauses. Second, the time limit for
IC3
was set to 30 sec. Third,
instead of constraining the number of PQE problems per benchmark
(i.e. the number of single clauses taken out of
\exists{\mbox{$X_{k}$}}[F_{k}]
)
like in the first experiment, we imposed the following two
constraints. First, we stopped processing a benchmark as soon as the
total of 100 free clauses was generated (for all the PQE problems
generated for this benchmark). Second, we stopped processing a
benchmark even earlier if the summary run time for all PQE problems
generated for this benchmark exceeded 2,000 sec.

A sample of 9 benchmarks out of the 98 we used in the experiment with

\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
is shown in Table
6
. Let us explain the structure
of this table by the benchmark 6s306 (the first line of the
table). The name of this benchmark is shown in the first column. The
second column gives the number of latches (7,986). The number of
invariants that should hold for 6s306 is provided in the third column
(25). So, the aggregate invariant
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
of 6s306 is the
conjunction of those 25 invariants. The fourth column shows that the
number
k
of time frames for 6s306 was set to 2 (since

|F_{3}|\!>\!500,000
). The value 182 shown in the fifth column is the
total number of single clauses taken out of
\exists{\mbox{$X_{k}$}}[F_{k}]

i.e. the number of PQE problems (where
k=2
for 6s306).

Every free clause
Q
generated by
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
when taking a clause out of

\exists{\mbox{$X_{k}$}}[F_{k}]
was stored as a local single-clause invariant.
The sixth column shows that taking clauses out of the scope of
quantifiers was terminated when 100 free clauses (specifying 100 local
single-clause invariants) were generated. Each of these 100 local
invariants held in
k
-th time frame. The following three columns show
how many of those 100 local invariants were true globally.
IC3
finished every problem out of 100 in the time limit of 30 sec. So, the
number of undecided invariants was 0. The number of invariants
IC3
proved false or true globally was 94 and 6 respectively. The last
column gives the number of global invariants
not
implied by

\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
i.e. invariants that may be unwanted. For 6s306, this
number is 6.

0.H.3
Experiment 3

To prove an invariant
P
true,
IC3
conjoins it with clauses

Q_{1},\dots,Q_{n}
to make
P\wedge Q_{1}\!\wedge\dots\wedge Q_{n}

inductive. If
IC3
succeeds, every
Q_{i}
is an invariant. Moreover,

Q_{i}
may be an
unwanted
invariant. Arguably, the cause of
efficiency of
IC3
is that
P
is often close to an inductive
invariant. So,
IC3
needs to generate a relatively small number of
clauses
Q_{i}
to make the constrained version of
P

inductive. However, this nice feature of
IC3
drastically limits the
set of invariant clauses it generates. In this subsection, we
substantiate this claim by an experiment. In this experiment, we
picked the HWMCC-13 benchmarks for which one could prove
all

predefined invariants
P_{1},\dots,P_{m}
within a time limit. Namely,
for every benchmark we formed the aggregate invariant
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
=

P_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge P_{m}
and ran
IC3
to prove
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
true.

We selected the benchmarks that
IC3
solved in less than 1,000 sec.
(In addition to dropping the benchmarks not solved in 1,000 sec., we
discarded those where
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
failed because some invariants
P_{i}
were
false). Let
P^{*}_{\mathit{agg}}
denote

the inductive version of
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
produced by
IC3
when proving
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
true. That is,
P^{*}_{\mathit{agg}}
is
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
conjoined with the invariant clauses produced by
IC3
.

For each of the selected benchmarks we generated invariants by
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
exactly as in Experiment 2. That is, we stopped generation of local
single clause invariants when their number exceeded 100. Then we ran

IC3
to identify local invariants that were global as well. After
that we checked which of the global invariants generated by
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
were
not implied by
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
. The difference from Experiment 2 was that we also
checked which global invariants generated by
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
were not implied by

P^{*}_{\mathit{agg}}
.

The results of the experiment are shown in Table
7
.
The first three columns of this table are the same as in
Table
6
. They give the name of a benchmark, the number
of latches and the number of invariants
P_{1}
,
\dots
,
P_{m}
to
prove. (The actual names of examples
ex1
,..,
ex4
in the
HWMCC-13 set are
pdtvsarmultip
,
bobtuintmulti
,

nusmvdme1d3multi
,
nusmvdme2d3multi
respectively.) The next
column of Table
7
shows the number of local invariants
generated by
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
that turn out to be global. The last two columns
give the number of global invariants that were not implied by
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
and

P^{*}_{\mathit{agg}}
respectively. The last row of the table shows that in 522 cases
out of 590 the invariants not implied by
\mathit{P}_{\mathit{\!agg}}
were not implied by

P^{*}_{\mathit{agg}}
either. So, in 88% of cases, the invariant clauses generated by

\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
were
different
from those generated by
IC3
to form
P^{*}_{\mathit{agg}}
.

Appendix 0.I
Experiment With Combinational Circuits

In this appendix, we give more information about the experiment with
property generation for combinational circuits described in
Section
9
. We formed PQE problems as follows. For
each benchmark
N
we picked the number
k
of time frames to unroll.
The value of
k
ranged from 10 to 40. (For larger circuits we picked
a smaller value of
k
.) Then we unrolled
N
for
k
time frames to
form a combinational circuit
M_{k}
and randomly generated a clause

B(S_{k})
of 15 literals. So,
B
depended on output variables of

M_{k}
. After that, we constructed the subcircuit
M^{\prime}_{k}
of
M_{k}
as
described in Section
9
. That is,
M^{\prime}_{k}
was
obtained by removing the logic of
M_{k}
that did not feed any output
variable present in
B
.

Let formula
F^{\prime}_{k}
specify the subcircuit
M^{\prime}_{k}
. (Here we reuse the
notation of Section
9
.) For every benchmark, we
generated PQE problems of taking different clauses
C
out of

\exists{S_{1,k}}[F^{\prime}_{k}]
. That is each PQE problem was to find
H
such
that
\mbox{$\exists{S_{1,k}}[F^{\prime}_{k}]$}\equiv H\wedge\mbox{$\exists{S_{1,k}}[F^{\prime}_{k}\setminus\mbox{$\{C\}$}]$}
. Each clause
C
to take out was chosen among the
clauses of
F^{\prime}_{k}
that contained a variable of
S_{k}
(i.e. an output
variable of
M^{\prime}_{k}
). In this way, we formed a set of 3,254 PQE
problems. 1,668 of these problems were solved by simple formula
preprocessing i.e. turned out to be trivial. So, in the experiment we
used the remaining 1,586 non-trivial PQE problems.

Table 8:
Property generation

of pqe

ber

%

%

The time limit for solving a PQE problem was set to 10 sec. Besides,
solving a PQE problem terminated as soon as the size of
H
reached 5
clauses. The results of the experiment are summarized in
Table
8
. The second column gives the total number
of PQE problems. The next two columns show the number and percentage
of problems where
H
was non-empty i.e. had at least one
clause. (Recall, that each clause of
H
represents a property.) The
last two columns give the number and percentage of cases where a
clause of
H
represented a property that was stronger than ones
produced by 3-valued simulation, a version of symbolic
simulation
[
22
]
. Consider, for instance, the last line of
the table corresponding to
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
. For 817 out of 1,046 PQE problems
where
H
was not empty, at least one clause of
H
constituted a
property that could not be produced by 3-valued
simulation. Table
8
shows that
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}
had the
weakest results generating properties only for 28% of problems
whereas
\mathit{DS}
-
\mathit{PQE}
and
\mathit{EG}
-
\mathit{PQE}^{+}
performed much better producing properties for
62% and 66% problems respectively.
